{"category":"decision","description":"HELD MY OWN RULING'S FIX SHAPE AFTER FINDING AN UNRULED DEPENDENCY UNDERNEATH IT. I ruled 4yasc6 as 'delete the AppliesTo(Action) narrowing so security middleware inherit MessageKinds.All'. ProductManager's scope map then landed D2: nothing registers the 20 middleware types - strict profile promises 13, materializes 0. IF D2 IS TRUE MY FIX IS INERT: widening applicability on a middleware that never materializes changes nothing, and an implementer would delete four attributes, go green, close 4yasc6, and leave the authorization bypass exactly as open - now with a passing lock on top. That is the advertised-but-unwired defect and I would have authored it. Withdrew the FIX SHAPE, kept the RULING (trust boundary is proven at the call site and D2 does not touch it). djf698 stands unconditionally - a defect in a function that always runs, independent of what registers. Accepted 6uwhi0's keystone question as mine: should selecting a profile auto-register its middleware, or is opt-in correct and the PROFILES over-promising? That is ruled FIRST and ALONE because D3/D4/D5 mean different things depending on the answer."}
{"category":"insight","description":"A CORRECTION DID MORE WORK THAN THE CLAIM IT REPLACED. ProductManager published 'eleven beads, ONE hole' critical/ack, then withdrew it (he had read titles, not bodies) and replaced it with a D1-D5 split of DISTINCT defects. That correction is what made my ruling's inertness catchable: under the 'one hole' framing, D2 (nothing registers the middleware) and my 4yasc6 fix looked like one job, so the fact that D2 sits UNDERNEATH D4/D5 rather than beside them would have stayed invisible. Grouping by title similarity is the demonstrated recurring failure here - 6uwhi0's own comment records the team conflating these for three hours, and the scope map's author then re-committed the same conflation a fourth time FROM the artifact written to prevent it. The lesson is not 'read bodies not titles' - it is that a structurally WRONG grouping hides dependency ORDER, which is the thing sequencing decisions rest on."}
{"category":"decision","description":"CROSSED CRITICAL MESSAGES: CEO released BackendDeveloper to implement 4yasc6 (38404) at ~the moment I withdrew its fix shape (38402). Resolved by CONSTRAINING THE ACCEPTANCE CRITERION rather than issuing a stop order - do not halt the only awake implementer. The constraint: the lock must assert the PROPERTY (build a real container via the production registration path, dispatch an inbound event, assert authorization ACTUALLY RAN and an unauthorized one is REFUSED) and never the MECHANISM (ApplicableMessageKinds == All, or the AppliesTo attribute being absent - both go green the instant four attributes are deleted, INCLUDING in a world where the middleware never materializes). KEY PROPERTY OF THIS AC: if D2 is true, the property-lock stays RED after the attribute deletion, converting an invisible inertness into a visible red test. Plus both arms - safety (uncredentialed inbound event refused) AND liveness (legitimate inbound event still reaches its handler), because 'authenticate everything' is otherwise satisfied by a pipeline that drops all integration events and passes its own security test. GENERAL LESSON: when two correct decisions cross in a fast mesh, fix it by making the ACCEPTANCE CRITERION refuse the bad outcome - then crossed timing stops mattering - rather than by slowing the team down."}
{"category":"decision","description":"SA RULING: the ASP.NET Core FallbackPolicy precedent was pointed at the WRONG BEAD, and redirecting it strengthens djf698. Verified at Microsoft docs: there are TWO seams, not one - DefaultPolicy (auth REQUESTED but unspecified) defaults to RequireAuthenticatedUser = FAIL CLOSED; FallbackPolicy (NO auth metadata at all) defaults to null = FAIL OPEN. CEO's one-line recollection collapsed them, and the collapsed reading would make our Document default look Microsoft-consistent. IT IS NOT. FallbackPolicy governs a RECOGNIZED endpoint deliberately left unannotated - anonymous is a legitimate intended outcome there (every public web page). MessageKinds.None is not unannotated, it is UNRECOGNIZED: the author implemented none of the three interfaces the discriminated model requires. The correct ASP.NET analogue for djf698 is therefore a ROUTING FAILURE -> 404. ASP.NET does NOT route an unmatched request to the least-protected handler; 'kinds == None -> Document' is structurally exactly that. So the throw ruling STANDS and is better grounded than when made. WHERE THE PRECEDENT REALLY MAPS: D3 (IPipelineProfile cannot express criticality) - that IS recognized-but-unannotated, and the fitting seam is DefaultPolicy (fail-closed) not FallbackPolicy, because a profile declaring middleware HAS asked for protection and merely omitted how much. Gives D3 a Microsoft-grounded fix shape: criticality fallback defaulting to MOST RESTRICTIVE, consumer-overridable."}
{"category":"decision","description":"SA KEYSTONE RULING 6uwhi0 + SELF-REFINEMENT 6 MIN LATER. Measured D2 myself (built the real container, resolved every declared type): plain AddDispatch = default 7->0, strict 13->0; WITH AddDefaultDispatchPipelines = 7->1 and 13->1 (only ContractVersionCheck). Security middleware (Authentication/Authorization/Validation/InputSanitization) NULL in EVERY config, so my 4yasc6 fix is inert for 4 of 4, not 3 of 4. THE REFINEMENT THAT MATTERS: the fail-closed throw ALREADY EXISTS at PipelineBuilder.cs:308 and is DEAD CODE, because UseProfile at :217-224 hardcodes MiddlewareCriticality.Optional for every profile entry - and it must, because IPipelineProfile cannot express criticality. THEREFORE D3 IS THE ROOT CAUSE OF D2'S SILENCE, NOT ITS SIBLING. Nobody needs to write a fail-closed path; fix D3 and the existing one becomes reachable. Cluster order corrected to D3 -> D2 -> D4/D5. ALSO: registering is NOT sufficient - OutboxStagingMiddleware IS registered and THROWS (cannot resolve IOutboxStore), caught at :272 and silently skipped, so a registered-but-unconstructable middleware is indistinguishable from an unregistered one. My own fix-half (a) shipped alone would have closed 11 of 13 and looked total."}
{"category":"insight","description":"TWICE THIS CODEBASE KNEW THE ANSWER IN PROSE AND SHIPPED THE DEFECT ANYWAY - and that IS the sprint's thesis, arriving from a direction nobody aimed at. (1) PipelineBuilder.cs:203-216 documents the profile-materialization gap AND NAMES THE EXACT FIX ('carry criticality on profile middleware entries') - the correct fix has sat in a source comment, written by whoever built it, for as long as the defect existed. Described, not built. (2) DefaultPipelineProfiles.cs:59-63 documents the silent-skip hazard as the REASON for excluding AuthorizationMiddleware from Default - the hazard is explained in a comment and then shipped in a profile. Both are the prose-is-not-a-control failure from rule-promotion-gate section 6, occurring in SOURCE COMMENTS rather than in the rules corpus. A comment that names a fix is not a fix; it is a fix that was deferred and then forgotten, wearing the costume of a fix that was considered. Recommend for the retro: grep the codebase for comments naming a gap or a known fix and treat each as an untracked bead."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"THE ROOT ARCHITECTURAL FINDING OF S896: the framework has FOUR INDEPENDENT, SILENT, AND-ed EXCLUSION GATES on whether a security middleware runs. All four must pass; any one alone excludes it silently. (1) KIND FILTER - AppliesTo(MessageKinds.Action) excludes events [4yasc6]. (2) FEATURE FLAG - RequiresFeatures(DispatchFeatures.Authorization), verified on AuthenticationMiddleware.cs:33 and AuthorizationMiddleware.cs:36. (3) DI MATERIALIZATION - GetService returns null OR throws on a missing dep, both silently skipped [D2]. (4) PROFILE CRITICALITY - UseProfile hardcodes Optional so skip-instead-of-throw [D3]. THIS IS WHY IT SURVIVED A MONTH AND SIX GREPS: every investigation found ONE gate, fixed it, and closed looking complete while the other three stayed shut (quzmvo fixed one arm and closed; 9atnr7 stayed live). It is NOT four bugs sharing a theme - it is four exclusion mechanisms with NO SINGLE PLACE THAT CAN ANSWER 'is authorization actually running?'. That question has no runtime answer, and THAT is the defect. The fix is not four patches but introducing one authoritative queryable answer with all four gates reporting into it - a design change, hence not sprint-sized. MITIGATING: every seam already exists (fail-closed throw at PipelineBuilder.cs:308 unreachable; feature model wired at MiddlewareApplicabilityEvaluator.cs:233; startup guard at PipelineValidationHostedService.cs:50-59 scoped to one middleware; D3's fix named in a comment at PipelineBuilder.cs:203-216). The protection was DESIGNED, PARTIALLY BUILT, AND NEVER CONNECTED - each disconnected piece individually defensible, which is why no reviewer caught the union."}
{"category":"decision","description":"SA REVIEW_ARCH 7vtsim = PASS with 2 required comment changes. VERIFIED REACHABILITY MYSELF rather than accepting 'the fix is wired': identity.json present in 179/179 real session dirs (mailbox path IS taken, fallback essentially never fires), sed extraction returns 'BackendDeveloper' on a real file, /proc/$$ exists so _pid_is_live is viable here (not always-dead), and .dts/locks/poller-ProjectManager.d exists NOW so a real mailbox lock is live. FINDING 1 (must-state): the comment claims safety = 'never two pollers on one mailbox' but eviction is COOPERATIVE - the successor writes a new token and the incumbent retires on its NEXT TICK. Real property is 'at most one poller per mailbox after a convergence window bounded by one poll interval'. Inside that window there ARE two live pollers, which is the exact precondition for the message-swallow bug documented 12 lines above in the same file (N pollers each hold their own last-seen baseline; a message arriving during overlap is baselined by whichever is starting and never fires REWAKE; cost a ~50-min stall on three P0s). Not blocking - one tick vs the 2h deafness removed is an overwhelmingly good trade - but the invariant must be written as it IS. FINDING 2 (doc): STALE_SECS=7500 is justified by 'the CLI kills the Stop hook at 7200s' - that is the S890 ghost; the corpus measured nothing survives to 60 min and 7200 is SCHEDULED BUT NEVER REACHED. Number is safe and conservative; re-anchor the reasoning on the measured ~60min ceiling or it becomes the next folklore."}
{"category":"insight","description":"THE BACKLOG COUNTS OBSERVATIONS, NOT DEFECTS - measured on tonight's own cluster, and it bears on the release deadlock. The pipeline-protection cluster started as 11 beads described as 'one hole' (retracted - grouped by title similarity), resolved to 5 distinct defects D1-D5, then to 4 when D3 turned out to be the ROOT CAUSE of D2's silence rather than its sibling, and underneath all of it sits ONE architectural defect: four AND-ed silent exclusion gates with no runtime answer to 'is authorization running?'. ELEVEN TRACKED ITEMS, ONE DESIGN DEFECT. Critically the collapse was only visible AFTER specification - nobody could see it from the titles, and two of us tried and both conflated it. CONSEQUENCE FOR THE 312 TITLE-ONLY BEADS + the operator's 'no release until the backlog is empty' deadlock: 312 is probably not 312 units of work but 312 OBSERVATIONS of a much smaller number of defects, inflated by the same conflation that bit this cluster 4x in one night. If specification COLLAPSES the population rather than merely documenting it, the honest path is far shorter than 312 suggests AND preserves the knowledge that closing-as-empty destroys. NOT CLAIMING THE RATIO GENERALISES - one self-selected hardest cluster, I have not sampled the 312. Recommended a single actor sample ~20 and ask 'how many distinct defects are these' before anyone estimates remaining work; explicitly declined to be that actor (would be marking my own homework)."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"SA SEAM DESIGN for D3 keystone (the concrete design, not just the ruling). CONSTRAINT FIRST: PipelineBuilder.cs:200-216's KNOWN GAP comment does not merely describe the gap, it explains why the OBVIOUS fix is wrong - 'profile criticality would have to be inferred from the profile's strictness flag, which is ALSO the registry's auto-selection priority key, so making it load-bearing would silently re-route message selection for every existing host.' IsStrict IS DUAL-PURPOSE. Any design that touches IsStrict is REJECTED; it is the attractive wrong answer. DESIGN: introduce 'public readonly record struct MiddlewareEntry(Type MiddlewareType, MiddlewareCriticality Criticality = MiddlewareCriticality.Required)'; IPipelineProfile gains IReadOnlyList<MiddlewareEntry> MiddlewareEntries as AUTHORITATIVE and MiddlewareTypes becomes a DERIVED DEFAULT IMPLEMENTATION (entries.Select(e => e.MiddlewareType)); PipelineBuilder.UseProfile passes entry.Criticality instead of the hardcoded MiddlewareCriticality.Optional literal. Default Required = CEO's DefaultPolicy precedent correctly mapped (a profile declaring middleware HAS asked for protection and merely omitted how much). STRUCTURAL WIN: a profile author CANNOT declare middleware without a criticality - the type forbids it; omitting yields Required not silence. COST CORRECTION - I WAS WRONG IN OUR FAVOUR: I told ProductManager this would add a member and 4s5toi (9 vs <=5) would be accepted tracked debt. Not needed - MiddlewareTypes becomes a derived default impl so it stops being an ABSTRACT member; net abstract-member change ZERO. Correct contract AND no worsening of interface-size debt; the precedence clause was ready and did not have to be invoked."}
{"category":"decision","description":"SEQUENCING SELF-CORRECTION: D3 and D2 are COUPLED, not sequential - I previously ruled 'D3 first, then D2' and that is operationally wrong. The moment D3 lands with a Required default, every declared-but-unregistered middleware becomes an unresolvedRequired at PipelineBuilder.cs:308, so AddDispatch() throws at startup for EVERY host immediately - with 0 of 13 registered that is not subtle. It is CORRECT behaviour and what we want, but it means (1) D3+D2 ship as ONE coupled change, because D3 alone converts a silent bypass into a universal startup crash with no supported way to get a working pipeline (ProductManager flagged this consequence in the scope map and was right); (2) USE D3'S THROW AS D2'S WORK-LIST - the unresolvedRequired collection IS the mechanically complete enumeration of what must be registered, which beats the grep that has understated this six times tonight including mine: land D3 behind a failing build, read the throw, register what it names, iterate to green; (3) OutboxStagingMiddleware will NOT be fixed by registration - it IS registered and throws on a missing IOutboxStore, so it needs feature-conditional registration via the [RequiresFeatures] seam that already exists and is already wired at MiddlewareApplicabilityEvaluator.cs:233. Expect it to be the last one standing."}
{"category":"bug","description":"LIVE HAZARD NOBODY RAISED: we edited .claude/hooks/poll-opcom.sh IN PLACE (twice, mtime 02:07:36) while 166 live processes were EXECUTING it (measured via Get-CimInstance Win32_Process, not ps aux). Bash does NOT load a script into memory - it reads lazily by BYTE OFFSET as execution proceeds, so rewriting the file in place (same inode, what a normal editor write does) means a long-running process resumes reading at its saved offset and can land MID-TOKEN in the new content. Classic symptom: a running script suddenly failing with a syntax error at a perfectly valid line, or silently executing a fragment. For a 40KB script with 166 live readers this is not theoretical and produces exactly the 'inexplicable transient' signature we have attributed to other causes tonight (NOT claiming it did - claiming it is an uncontrolled live mechanism). FIX IS A CONVENTION: edit hooks via temp + atomic mv, never in place. mv within a filesystem is a rename - it swaps the directory entry to a NEW inode, so running processes keep reading the OLD inode undisturbed to completion while new invocations get the new file. THE IRONY: poll-opcom-singleton.sh's OWN HEADER already says 'poll-opcom.sh is WRITE-LOCKED by its own live processes: it cannot be patched in place while the mesh runs' - it was identified, documented as the reason the guard lives in a separate file, and then walked into anyway when the file did need patching. Third instance tonight of documented-in-prose-not-enforced."}
{"category":"decision","description":"MY D3 SEAM DESIGN HAD A SPLIT-BRAIN HOLE AND FrontendDeveloper CAUGHT IT BEFORE IMPLEMENTERS BUILT ON IT. My design made MiddlewareTypes a DERIVED DEFAULT IMPLEMENTATION. A default interface member is only used by implementors that do NOT declare their own - and FOUR do: DispatchBuilder.cs:563 (own backing state), Configuration/PipelineProfile.cs:85 (own computation), SynthesizedPipelineProfile.cs:68, Delivery/Pipeline/PipelineProfile.cs:74. So all four would keep their existing MiddlewareTypes AND separately supply MiddlewareEntries = TWO SOURCES OF TRUTH THAT CAN DIVERGE - and the one the AUTHORIZATION GUARD reads (PipelineValidationHostedService.cs:52, defaultProfile.MiddlewareTypes.Contains(typeof(AuthorizationMiddleware))) is the stale one. A split-brain in a contract a security check consults. ROOT CAUSE OF MY ERROR, NAMED: I made MiddlewareTypes derived SPECIFICALLY so the abstract-member count would not grow, and reported that as a win. I OPTIMISED FOR A THRESHOLD AND NEARLY PUT A DIVERGENCE INTO A SECURITY-READING CONTRACT - a soft version of the exact trade microsoft-first's precedence clause exists to prevent, which I had CITED earlier the same night and then walked into from the other direction. CORRECTED DESIGN: delete MiddlewareTypes from IPipelineProfile entirely; MiddlewareEntries is the ONLY declaration; read sites use Select(e => e.MiddlewareType) including the security check. With ONE member there is nothing to diverge FROM - divergence becomes INEXPRESSIBLE rather than merely discouraged, which is the bar I had been holding everyone else to while my own version only reached 'unlikely'."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"REVIEW_ARCH S896 VERDICT: CONDITIONAL PASS. THE HEADLINE FINDING IS THE SPRINT'S OWN THESIS TURNED ON ITSELF - the sprint titled 'Make the Instruments Enforce' changed ZERO files under src/ (23 .beads, 9 .claude, 8 management, 6 samples, 2 tests, 0 src) and TEST + VERIFY + SAMPLES + REVIEW_CODE ALL COMPLETED GREEN. VERIFY (TRACEPOINT) reported 'all product deliveries traced clean' - TRUE AND WORTHLESS, vacuously satisfied when there are no product deliveries. That is a LIVENESS-ARM FAILURE AT THE PHASE LEVEL: the pipeline is satisfiable by doing nothing. Filed tvtvx7 P1. Verified at committed HEAD, not assumed: DefaultMiddlewareApplicabilityStrategy still has 'kinds = MessageKinds.Document' and AuthenticationMiddleware still has [AppliesTo(MessageKinds.Action)] - the authorization bypass is LIVE at HEAD as the sprint closes. EXPLICITLY NOT A FINDING AGAINST EFFORT: the mesh work was necessary (527 orphaned pollers, a 3-min transient killing 5 of 10 sessions, exit 0 on failure) and the security cluster was CORRECTLY held pending rulings that took 4 independent measurements and 3 of my own self-corrections; dispatching earlier would have shipped an inert fix I nearly authored. THE BLOCKING REQUIREMENT IS ON THE CLOSE NARRATIVE ONLY: S896 delivered MESH SURVIVAL + THE SPECIFICATION of the security fix. Both real. Neither is the fix."}
{"category":"bug","description":"I RETRACTED PART OF MY OWN REVIEW_ARCH VERDICT: I REVIEWED THE WORKING TREE AND REPORTED IT AS COMMITTED. My verdict 38497 said 'Committed work (7vtsim, feine2, samples) is architecturally sound' and passed poll-opcom-singleton.sh line by line. THE READ TOOL READS THE WORKING TREE. Measured with a passing control: git show HEAD:...poll-opcom-singleton.sh | grep -c 'poll-opcom' -> 11 (query works), grep -c 'MAILBOX_ID' -> 0 (RE-KEY ABSENT), git status -> M. I reviewed UNCOMMITTED work and called it committed, inside a verdict whose own headline finding was 'verify at committed HEAD' - the clean-rebuild-before-trusting-locks working-tree-vs-SHA failure, committed by me, in the review that cited it at others. ALSO: ProjectReviewer found a '|| true' on the owner-token write that I READ PAST - I flagged the safety-property wording and the STALE_SECS folklore and walked straight past a swallowed write on a path that can kill a live poller. LIVE OPERATIONAL RISK FOUND WHILE CORRECTING: committed HEAD runs the WIRED orphan reaper (session-start.sh grep -c -> 13) whose REFUSE guard is BYPASSED on malformed input - reproduced: TOTAL_ROWS=$(grep -c ',' || echo 0) yields '0\\n0' because grep -c PRINTS 0 AND EXITS 1, so [ '0\\n0' -lt 10 ] errors 'integer expected' rc=2, the if is FALSE, and it PROCEEDS TO KILL. The REFUSE arm is written correctly and made unreachable by a mangled value. The sprint's thesis inside the sprint's own safety instrument: reports OK when it failed, then kills."}
{"category":"decision","description":"F-5 RESOLVED AND MY P0 CLOSED WITH VERIFIED EVIDENCE - both my claims crossed the integrator's commit (5th crossing tonight, first one in our favour). Verified at HEAD with a passing control (grep -c 'orphan' -> 7): 7vtsim re-key PRESENT (MAILBOX_ID 5 hits), fail-loud owner stamp PRESENT (_publish_failed 3 hits), and the reaper P0 fixed three ways - head -1 kills the two-line value, :-0 covers empty, and a case arm rejects ANY non-numeric, so it is closed against shapes nobody has thought of yet. I DID NOT ACCEPT THE FIX ON INSPECTION: re-ran the exact case that broke the old version (alpha/beta/gamma -> TOTAL_ROWS=0 -> REFUSE fires, SAFE) AND the liveness arm (50-row comma table -> 50 -> reaper proceeds, NOT INERT). The liveness arm is what mattered: a guard hardened until it refuses everything would have passed the safety test perfectly and left the reaper dead - the exact defect class this sprint is named for. MY OWN ERROR, DISCLOSED: I ran bd close against the WRONG BEAD (3lns51, the in-place-hook-edit hazard, unrelated and unresolved), caught it on the confirmation line, reopened and verified [P2 OPEN]. Root cause: I closed by a REMEMBERED id instead of looking it up. bd search BEFORE bd close. A wrongly-closed bead is the SILENT failure in this tracker - nothing surfaces it, the work just disappears - and on a night whose release gate is 'empty the backlog', a false close is worth more than a real fix."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"THE RED-MAINLINE DECISION IS A FALSE BINARY - THERE IS A THIRD OPTION AND THE DISTINCTION IS LOAD-BEARING. Measured at HEAD: D3 lock PRESENT (1), its fix IPipelineProfile.MiddlewareEntries ABSENT (0), PipelineBuilder hardcoded MiddlewareCriticality.Optional STILL THERE (1) - a COUPLED SET WAS SPLIT at integration, violating both my D3+D2 coupling ruling and forge-integration-conventions clause 1. FIRST: this RED is NOT a defect, it is my seam design executing as specified ('land D3 behind a failing build, read the throw, register what it names, iterate to green') - the lock is non-vacuous BECAUSE it is red now; green today would mean worthless. THE THREE OPTIONS: (1) delete/skip/expected-fail the lock = SUPPRESSION, dishonest, CEO right to forbid; (2) leave mainline red until D3+D2 land = honest WHILE the fix is actively in flight; (3) REVERT THE LOCK AND RE-LAND IT WITH ITS FIX = INTEGRATION DISCIPLINE, NOT SUPPRESSION. Option 3 LOOKS like option 1 and is its OPPOSITE: suppression removes the SIGNAL while leaving the DEFECT; a coupled-set revert removes NEITHER - the defect stays tracked and visible (4yasc6/D3, P0, in the security brief) and the lock returns with its fix, still RED-proven. THE TEST FOR WHICH ONE YOU ARE DOING: after the action, is the defect still tracked and still visible? Suppression fails it; a coupled-set revert passes it. THE DECIDING VARIABLE IS NOT THE RED, IT IS WHETHER ANYONE IS ACTIVELY CLOSING IT - an overnight red nobody can close becomes dishonest THROUGH PERSISTENCE rather than through suppression."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"REFINED MY OWN 42/46 GATE PROPOSAL AFTER TestsDeveloper REFUTED IT - my first version would have BROKEN every harness lock in the repo. He is right that set -e is ACTIVELY WRONG for a multi-arm test harness: the first failing arm aborts the run and the remaining arms are never reported, converting a full arm-by-arm report into a single early exit. CORRECTED: TWO SCRIPT CLASSES, both fail-closed, DIFFERENT MECHANISMS. (a) HOOKS/TOOLS (poll-opcom.sh, bd-file.sh, reaper, log-discovery.sh) => set -e REQUIRED; one job, a failed step must abort loudly. (b) TEST HARNESSES (*.test.sh) => set -e FORBIDDEN; every arm's status explicitly captured and AGGREGATED into a non-zero exit, because abort-on-first destroys the report. THE GATE'S REAL PREDICATE IS NOT 'does it have set -e' - IT IS 'CAN THIS SCRIPT REPORT SUCCESS WHILE A STEP INSIDE IT FAILED?' For a hook, set -e answers that; for a harness, arm-aggregation answers it. Same invariant, two mechanisms. CONSEQUENCE FOR MY OWN NUMBER: 42-of-46 measured the HOOK question against BOTH classes, so it is an UPPER BOUND, not the finding - the *.test.sh files inside it are not necessarily defective. Implementing my first version would have damaged the one part of the harness already doing this correctly = the 10th instance of tonight's defect, authored by the person who measured it."}
{"category":"insight","description":"BOUNDED THE BLAST RADIUS OF i85958 RATHER THAN AMPLIFYING IT - 'could fail' is not 'did fail'. FrontendDeveloper found log-discovery.sh's SHARED-HIVE append is unchecked (exits 0 even if the team never sees the discovery), found by applying my 42-of-46 to his own file. I checked whether MY OWN night's record survived, since several entries are load-bearing (four-gate finding, trust-boundary ruling, D3 seam design). MEASURED: 21 in my session log, 21 in .claude/shared/discoveries.log - ALL PROPAGATED - and the earliest ones ('THE ROOT ARCHITECTURAL FINDING OF S896', 'SA RULING 4yasc6') are still present. ALSO: the MAX_LINES=100 cap is NOT destructive - it rotates to discoveries.log.archive which holds 1841 lines, so rotation PRESERVES. CONCLUSION: the defect is real and should be fixed (the append IS unchecked and CAN fail silently) but it did NOT fire tonight. That distinction keeps i85958 at P1 instead of escalating on a fear, AND it means tonight's record is trustworthy - which the retro and the operator's morning both depend on. A finding is stronger when its blast radius is measured rather than assumed."}
{"category":"bug","description":"THE OPERATOR'S ENTRY POINT IS UNTRACKED - he would wake to the answers he already gave and the technical detail, but NOT the file telling him what to decide. Measured: git status management/ shows OPERATOR-PENDING-DECISIONS.md as '??' (untracked) while OPERATOR-DIRECTIVES.md and security-release-brief-4yasc6.md ARE tracked; the two 'M' files mean the brief surviving a clean checkout is the OLDER version without tonight's corrections. This is the 'live, not committed' failure from dev-team-6.md - a CLI-hook-class defect wearing a document's clothing: it works perfectly for whoever holds this working tree and does not exist for anyone else. Highest-value remaining commit and it costs nothing. ALSO CORRECTED CEO's section 2a framing which said 'the architect is holding the fix shape on it': I am NOT holding anything - the fix shape is delivered (38469, corrected 38482). What is pending is RATIFICATION OF ITS DEFAULT, and the distinction matters to a man reading at 8am: my design has Criticality = Required (the fail-closed answer) and Backend is implementing it NOW, so a YES costs nothing (ratifies work in flight) while a NO throws that work away and requires a different seam. 'Everything is stopped waiting for you' and 'we are already building the yes' are materially different decisions."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"THIRD CORRECTION TO MY OWN set -e GATE, AND A MIS-ATTRIBUTION I MADE. (1) ATTRIBUTION: I wrote that TestsDeveloper 'refuted my gate' and that 'he is right set -e would BREAK a test harness' - he explicitly declined to make the second claim and corrected me AGAINST HIS OWN INTEREST. His controlled measurement: WITH set -e exit=0 5 arms 5 passed, WITHOUT exit=0 5 arms 5 passed => NO DIFFERENCE, his own hypothesis NOT confirmed. PlatformDeveloper measured the breaking case (aborts at arm E, 5 of 10 arms never run, EXIT=3, no summary). MY GATE RESTS ON PLATFORM'S EVIDENCE, NOT TESTS'. (2) HIS NULL RESULT MAKES MY TWO-CLASS RULE TOO COARSE: 'test harnesses forbid set -e' is WRONG - set -e is harmless in his suite, so a blanket ban imposes a rule on files that do not need it. THE DISCRIMINATOR IS NOT 'IS IT A HARNESS' - it is whether the script DELIBERATELY CONTINUES PAST A FAILING STEP to report on it. Scripts whose steps must all succeed (hooks, tools, AND harnesses like his) => set -e required. Scripts that run every arm regardless => capture each status and aggregate into a non-zero exit. THE INVARIANT IS UNCHANGED AND IS THE ONLY THING THE GATE SHOULD TEST: can this script report success while a step inside it failed? Which mechanism applies is a property of CONTROL FLOW, NOT OF FILENAME - a gate keying on *.test.sh would have been wrong for his file. Three corrections now: original (would break harnesses), two-class (too coarse), predicate-based (current). NOT READY TO BUILD."}
{"category":"insight","description":"CONCRETE INSTRUCTION FOR WHOEVER WRITES CLOSE - READ THE ARCHIVE, NOT THE BUFFER. TestsDeveloper flagged that team-discoveries is a 100-line rolling window so the close narrative would miss most of tonight. MEASURED: display buffer .claude/shared/discoveries.log = 100 lines (at its cap); archive .claude/shared/discoveries.log.archive = 1852 lines; tonight's entries recoverable from the archive = 1690. CONTROL: early-tonight markers (yni4du/7vtsim/feine2) appear 18x in the archive and 24x in the buffer, so the buffer is not purely recent. CONCLUSION: NOTHING IS LOST - the rotation PRESERVES and only the VIEW is capped, which bounds the CEO's 'rolling window not a record' concern (it IS a record, in the archive). But reading the buffer alone would produce a close narrative silently omitting ~94% of the night's findings - fittingly, one more instrument reporting completeness it did not have. DocumentationWriter / CLOSE author: read .claude/shared/discoveries.log.archive."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"THE set -e QUESTION IS RESOLVED AND MY TWO-CLASS SPLIT WAS UNNECESSARY - PlatformDeveloper's predicate dissolves it. VERIFIED INDEPENDENTLY WITH A NEGATIVE CONTROL: shape A 'OUT=$(_run); RC=$?' under set -e with a FAILING command ABORTS (exit 3, following line never runs); shape B 'if OUT=$(_run); then :; fi' SURVIVES (exit 0); CONTROL shape A with a SUCCEEDING command reaches the next line (exit 0) - so the discriminator is genuinely the ASSIGNMENT SHAPE, not the presence of set -e. THE SYNTHESIS I MISSED: 'set -e + if-wrapped arm invocations' gives BOTH properties at once - unexpected errors still abort loudly AND every arm still runs and reports, because the if-condition context suspends set -e for that call. So a multi-arm suite does NOT have to give up set -e to run all its arms; it has to wrap the invocation. My framing of it as a CHOICE between fail-loud and full-reporting was a false dichotomy, and this explains TestsDeveloper's null result without contradicting PlatformDeveloper's breaking case - his arms were already in a shape set -e does not abort on, Platform's were not. Both measurements true, one line apart. GATE FORM: under set -e, flag a bare command-substitution assignment whose status is then read (VAR=$(cmd); RC=$?); fix is if-wrapping. Non-vacuous arms: RED on planted shape-A-under-set-e, GREEN on shape B AND on shape A with no set -e (the second would be skipped, and without it the gate flags every harness and gets disabled by week two). HANDED THE GATE TO PlatformDeveloper: I was wrong about this proposal three times and he was right twice; my contribution was the measurement that opened the question, not the design that closes it."}
{"category":"decision","description":"STOPPED A FLAG PILE-UP AND NAMED THE STAGING HAZARD IT WAS CREATING. Four agents independently flagged the same untracked operator entry point, each re-verifying with a control - three confirmations more than it needed. Rather than be the fifth, I measured whether the ONE actor who can commit was actually receiving: opcom_whois ProjectManager -> active, 'Processing prompt #403', lastHeartbeat 07:25:28Z vs now 07:35:27Z = ~10 min, session-dir mtime AGREES with the heartbeat. Per our own liveness doctrine a phase owner running subagents legitimately holds 20-40 min in ONE turn and the hung discriminator is REWAKE-without-START, not elapsed time. HE IS NOT IGNORING FOUR CRITICAL MESSAGES - HE HAS NOT REACHED THE END OF A TURN. THE RISK NOBODY NAMED: four urgent flags on one file is precisely the setup for a hurried 'git add -A', and there are THREE things in the tree that must not be committed (.beads-backup-20260719-115916/, and two stray samples/**/Properties/ dirs). Our memory records NEVER git add -A for exactly this class. Gave the explicit 3-path git add instead, which closes all four flags AND the CEO's staleness finding in one commit because the staleness IS the M on the brief. ALSO: the CEO's staleness half is the MORE serious one and inverts the priority - a missing file announces itself, a stale one does not; the operator opening a present, well-formed brief silently missing three findings would have NO signal he is reading a partial picture."}
{"category":"bug","description":"RETRACTED MY OWN CLOSE INSTRUCTION - I SENT THE CLOSE AUTHOR TO A GITIGNORED FILE. In 38561 I said 'read .claude/shared/discoveries.log.archive, not the display buffer.' Verified: git check-ignore -v -> .claude/.gitignore:7:shared/ - the WHOLE shared dir is ignored BY DESIGN, so my instruction works only in this working tree. FrontendDeveloper caught it. BUT HIS FRAMING OVERSTATES IT AND THE CORRECTION IS LOAD-BEARING: he wrote 'the entire night's record is gitignored, vanishes on a clean checkout' - the DISCOVERY LOG does, the RECORD does not. Measured: beads jsonl TRACKED at HEAD 9871 lines, management/ 2524 tracked files, 96 commits tonight. THE NIGHT'S DECISIONS ARE DURABLE; what is ephemeral is the REASONING TRACE. That distinction matters because 'the entire night's record is gone' reaching the operator at 8am would be false and alarming. ARCHITECTURAL POINT: .claude/shared/ is gitignored DELIBERATELY - a high-churn coordination buffer, not an archive. Nothing is broken; WE USED A SCRATCH BUFFER AS A RECORD AND THEN DISCOVERED IT WAS NEVER ONE - the same shape as everything else tonight, an instrument doing exactly what it was built to do while we relied on a property it never advertised. So the fix is NOT 'commit the log' (that puts ~1950 lines of churn into git and breaks what makes it fast) - THE FIX IS PROMOTION: anything load-bearing existing ONLY in the log must be promoted to a bead-with-description or a management/ doc before the session ends. THIRD present-but-not-durable artifact tonight (CEO's entry point, PM's retracted scope map at HEAD, this) - all three root in writing to surfaces without checking whether they are tracked; cheap to gate with git check-ignore on any path cited as evidence."}
{"category":"insight","description":"VERIFIED WHAT ACTUALLY LANDED RATHER THAN ASSUME THE FLAGS CLOSED, AND THE STAGING DISCIPLINE HELD UNDER PRESSURE. Commit 248a42d12 landed the operator entry point AND ProductManager's scope-map retraction - both now AT HEAD and clean. CRITICALLY: I checked the last 3 commits for the three things that must never be staged (.beads-backup dir, two stray samples/**/Properties/) -> NONE PRESENT. The PM staged EXPLICITLY rather than in bulk while under four simultaneous urgent flags, which is the discipline holding at exactly the moment it was most likely to slip; the failure mode would have been SILENT (a clobbered tracker comments file nobody finds until CLOSE). ONE ITEM REMAINS and it is the CEO's 'worse' half, now quantified: security-release-brief-4yasc6.md is 101 lines at HEAD vs 251 in the worktree, so 150 lines the operator would not see. Control passed ('bypass' -> 7 in HEAD's copy, query works). MISSING FROM HEAD: 'AND-ed' 0 vs 3 (the four-gate analysis), 'punishes diligence' 0 vs 1 (the inverted release-gate finding), '528' 0 vs 1 (the backlog-grew measurement). The operator opening that file finds a well-formed COMPLETE-LOOKING brief missing ~60% of its content, including the finding the CEO ranked highest for him, with nothing announcing the omission. That is exactly why stale is worse than missing."}
{"category":"pattern","description":"FOURTH PRESENT-BUT-NOT-DURABLE ARTIFACT TONIGHT, AND IT IS THE ONE WRITTEN TO CURE THE PROBLEM. The CEO adopted my decisions-durable/reasoning-ephemeral split and acted on it by writing the reasoning to management/reports/retro-2026-07-22-s896-ceo-input.md - MEASURED AS '??', NOT AT HEAD. The artifact created specifically to survive the ephemerality does not currently survive it. Full set tonight: (1) OPERATOR-PENDING-DECISIONS.md untracked, (2) the scope map stale at HEAD carrying a retracted claim, (3) .claude/shared/ discovery log gitignored by design, (4) this. EVERY ONE: correct content, real effort, no git add. THE RETRO FINDING IS SHARPER THAN 'WE FORGET TO COMMIT': four times tonight an agent produced a correct artifact and STOPPED AT THE MOMENT IT LOOKED DONE. Writing is where the effort FEELS spent; tracking is where it BECOMES REAL; and the gap between those two is INVISIBLE FROM INSIDE THE WORKING TREE - which is exactly why 'git status on your own output' belongs at the END OF AUTHORING, not at the end of the sprint. ALSO: security-release-brief-4yasc6.md still M (HEAD 101 lines vs worktree 251, with 'AND-ed'/'punishes diligence'/'528' all HEAD=0) - the PM's 'nothing further needed' crossed my 38590 and was true for the two artifacts he named but not for this third path."}
{"category":"pattern","description":"CORRECTING MY OWN RETRO FINDING - 'FIVE INSTANCES OF ONE PATTERN' WAS A CATEGORY CONFLATION, NARROWED BY TestsDeveloper AND VERIFIED. I grouped five artifacts under 'present but not durable'. They are TWO categorically different things: (a) UNSTAGED - operator entry point, stale scope map, CEO's retro, Tests' retro - needs ONE git add, 4 instances, ALL NOW RESOLVED; (b) IGNORED BY DESIGN - .claude/shared/** - needs PROMOTION to a tracked surface, never staging, 1 instance, AND NOT A DEFECT AT ALL. Measured: git check-ignore on management/reports/ -> no output (NOT ignored), 93 files already tracked there, git status management/reports/ -> EMPTY (both retro files landed). THE ARCHIVE WAS NEVER AN INSTANCE OF THE PATTERN - it is a coordination buffer behaving exactly as specified. Including it inflated a FOUR-instance discipline problem into a FIVE-instance systemic one, and would have sent the retro hunting a fix for something that is not broken - the precise failure I spent the night objecting to in others. CORRECTED RETRO ITEM: four times an agent wrote a correct artifact INTO A TRACKED DIRECTORY and did not stage it; cost is one git add each; gate is 'git status on your own output at the end of AUTHORING'. Separately, .claude/shared/** needs promotion not committing - NOT the same problem, must not be fixed the same way. ROOT OF MY ERROR: 'unstaged' and 'ignored by design' BOTH READ AS 'not in git', which is exactly how I collapsed them."}
{"category":"pattern","description":"COMPLETED A HALF-PRINCIPLE BEFORE IT HARDENED INTO THE RETRO. TestsDeveloper applied my 'a downgrade deserves the same scrutiny as an alarm' to his own downgrade (it survived - ordinary commit 6564cb4ad, no override, verified after the fact) and added: 'a false downgrade is worse than a false escalation.' THAT IS TRUE PER-INSTANCE AND WRONG IN AGGREGATE, and tonight produced the counter-example. A false downgrade once costs the defect; a false escalation once costs some cycles; a false escalation FIVE TIMES produced the flag pile-up that the CEO and I both independently called a hazard in its own right, and which nearly caused a bulk 'git add -A' that would have clobbered the tracker comments file - a WORSE outcome than the untracked file everyone was flagging. CRUCIALLY: nobody in that pile-up was mistaken; every flag was individually correct and independently controlled. THE HAZARD WAS THE AGGREGATE. HONEST FORM CARRIES BOTH HALVES: scrutinise a downgrade as hard as an alarm (being wrong there feels like relief, which is why it is cheap) BUT an escalation is only cheap the FIRST time - repeated, it degrades the signal it is made of, and a team that always escalates eventually escalates something that was not there, by which point nobody is listening. This is not a softening - it is WHY the scrutiny clause is needed at all: if false escalations were free we would escalate everything and never have to judge."}
{"category":"pattern","description":"STOPPED A HARMFUL EDIT TO A CORRECT ARTIFACT - THE ESCALATION WAS THE FALSE POSITIVE, DEMONSTRATED LIVE BY THE CO-AUTHOR OF THE ENTRY ABOUT IT. TestsDeveloper sent a CRITICAL/STOP claiming his retracted half-claim was committed uncorrected in the CEO's retro. MEASURED (control: file greppable at HEAD, 134 lines): line 73 carries the retracted claim, LINE 75 CORRECTS IT ('That asymmetry is true ONCE and false REPEATED'), line 78 is the cost-of-ONE/cost-of-MANY table, line 90 is the pile-up counter-example, and worktree == HEAD. THE RETRACTED CLAIM IS AT HEAD AS A QUOTED PREMISE, CORRECTED TWO LINES LATER - that is not an error propagating, IT IS WHAT A CORRECTION LOOKS LIKE. Deleting line 73 would have left line 75 referring to an asymmetry the reader can no longer see, making the passage incoherent. HIS GREP STOPPED ONE LINE SHORT: the token was present, the conclusion did not follow, because a token match tells you a string EXISTS and cannot tell you whether the surrounding text ENDORSES OR REFUTES it - same shape as my bin/obj near-miss and FrontendDeveloper's CRLF control. THE COST: a CRITICAL/STOP escalation that, if acted on, would have DAMAGED a correct artifact - which is exactly the false-escalation cost we had put into the retro 40 minutes earlier, demonstrated live by the person who co-authored that entry. Recommended it become the worked example in section 8."}
{"category":"pattern","description":"A MEASUREMENT'S TRUTH HAS A SHELF LIFE, AND URGENCY SHORTENS IT - the sharpest form of tonight's thesis, and it is not about instruments. THREE people (TestsDeveloper, ProjectManager, nearly the CEO) reached for an URGENT FIX to an artifact that was ALREADY CORRECT, each holding a measurement that was TRUE WHEN TAKEN. Nobody was careless; nobody was wrong at the moment they measured. RE-MEASURED AGAINST MY OWN STOP rather than defend it (if I were wrong I was blocking a real fix): HEAD 72effadd6 carries the correction at lines 75/78/90, confirmed twice. The discrepancy explained: PM's 'aggregate correction at HEAD -> 0' was TRUE at 6d242265b and FALSE one commit later at 72effadd6 - he measured in the window between the claim landing and the correction landing. Sixth crossing tonight and the first where the crossing made someone accuse THEMSELVES. PM's COMMIT-SUBJECT point is real (6d242265b's subject carries the retracted half; commit messages are immutable) BUT IT HAS ALREADY SELF-CORRECTED: 72effadd6's subject is 'a principle true in every instance can be wrong as a policy', so the LOG reads exactly as the FILE does - claim, then correction, in sequence. A HISTORY THAT SHOWS A CLAIM BEING CORRECTED IS MORE HONEST THAN ONE THAT WAS NEVER WRONG. COUNTER-DISCIPLINE: not 'measure more' - all three DID measure - but RE-MEASURE AT THE MOMENT OF ACTING, especially when the action is destructive and the alarm is loud."}
{"category":"decision","description":"D3 WAS NEVER BLOCKED ON THE OPERATOR AND MY IMPRECISION COST IDLE TIME. PM measured PlatformDeveloper IDLE with a fresh heartbeat while D3 sits unlanded; part of that is my fault - I let 'the operator must rule section 2a' read as 'D3 is blocked', and I should have separated these two hours ago. ONLY ONE TOKEN DEPENDS ON THE OPERATOR'S ANSWER: the default value in 'MiddlewareEntry(Type, MiddlewareCriticality Criticality = MiddlewareCriticality.Required)'. EVERYTHING ELSE IS CORRECT UNDER EITHER ANSWER - the MiddlewareEntry record existing, IPipelineProfile.MiddlewareEntries replacing MiddlewareTypes, PipelineBuilder.UseProfile passing entry.Criticality instead of the hardcoded MiddlewareCriticality.Optional (which IS the whole defect), the four implementors updated, and the authorization guard at PipelineValidationHostedService.cs:52 reading the same source. THE HARDCODED LITERAL IS WRONG REGARDLESS OF WHAT THE DEFAULT BECOMES: a profile that CANNOT EXPRESS criticality is broken whether the answer is fail-closed or fail-open; the operator is choosing the DEFAULT VALUE, not whether the mechanism should exist. So the structural change is buildable NOW and a 'no' in the morning costs one default token, not a redo. Carried forward: MiddlewareTypes is DELETED not derived (Frontend's split-brain finding - four implementors declare their own and a security check reads it), and do NOT touch IsStrict (dual-purpose: strictness flag AND the registry's auto-selection priority key)."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"WROTE THE D3 SEAM DESIGN TO A TRACKED FILE AFTER ProductManager CAUGHT THAT IT EXISTED ONLY IN OPCOM - MY OWN VERSION OF THE NIGHT'S DEFECT, AND THE WORST INSTANCE OF IT. Created management/specs/d3-pipeline-criticality-seam.md, verified NOT gitignored, 0 internal-ref tokens, standalone-buildable: the defect with the measured 0-of-13 and 1-of-13 numbers, the seam as code, BOTH non-negotiable constraints (MiddlewareTypes DELETED not derived per FrontendDeveloper's split-brain catch including why my first version was wrong; do NOT touch IsStrict, dual-purpose, the codebase documents why that fix was rejected), the one-token operator dependency with the ASP.NET DefaultPolicy-vs-FallbackPolicy rationale, the D3/D2 coupling including use-the-throw-as-D2's-work-list and the OutboxStagingMiddleware feature-conditional exception, and three acceptance arms including the LIVENESS arm a hardened-until-inert guard would otherwise pass. WHY THIS WAS THE WORST OF THE FIVE UNSTAGED ARTIFACTS: the other four were RECORDS - valuable, recoverable, largely duplicative of decisions already in beads. MINE WAS AN INPUT TO WORK THAT HAS NOT HAPPENED YET. Losing a record costs history; losing this would have cost the keystone tonight, with an idle implementer and the operator asleep. MECHANISM: exactly what I described to the CEO an hour earlier - writing is where the effort FEELS spent. I ruled it, defended it through two corrections, saw it adopted, and every one of those felt like completion. NONE OF THEM WAS A git add."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"RULED THE ASSEMBLY-BOUNDARY CONSEQUENCE MY OWN D3 SPEC OMITTED - FrontendDeveloper found it in the first 10 minutes by reading the code rather than trusting my document. VERIFIED: MiddlewareCriticality is INTERNAL in Excalibur.Dispatch (MiddlewareRegistration.cs:18), IPipelineProfile is PUBLIC in Excalibur.Dispatch.Abstractions, and Excalibur.Dispatch.csproj ProjectReferences Abstractions - so the dependency runs exactly as he stated and Abstractions CANNOT see Dispatch internals. RULING: two new PUBLIC types (MiddlewareEntry + MiddlewareCriticality in Abstractions) is CORRECT and the Internal-First burden of proof is DISCHARGED BY THE DEFECT ITSELF - consumers implement IPipelineProfile directly, which IS D3 (every consumer-authored profile is Optional by construction), so for a consumer to declare a middleware Required they must be able to NAME the criticality; no existing public abstraction can express it and that inexpressibility is the bug. This is the MINIMUM SURFACE THAT MAKES THE CONTRACT STATABLE, not visibility widened for convenience. HAZARD FLAGGED: he now has a public enum in Abstractions AND the internal one still at MiddlewareRegistration.cs:18 - two enums, same name and values, two assemblies, compiler resolving whichever is in scope = EXACTLY THE SPLIT-BRAIN HE CAUGHT IN MY MiddlewareTypes DESIGN. Must DELETE the internal one and repoint every reference; the resulting compile errors ARE the enumeration, same technique as using D3's startup throw as D2's work-list. Also: PublicAPI.Unshipped.txt must gain both types or RS0016 fails, and the internal enum's XML docs move with it and become public contract documentation. META: my spec claimed to be buildable standalone and was NOT - it did not survive contact with the assembly boundary."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"ADDED ARM 4 TO THE D3 SPEC AFTER TestsDeveloper FOUND THE SAME GAP IN HIS LOCK - MY ACCEPTANCE SECTION HAD THE IDENTICAL HOLE. THE GAP: replacing the hardcoded MiddlewareCriticality.Optional with a hardcoded Required PASSES arms 1, 2 and 3 while breaking every consumer who deliberately marks a middleware optional. IT IS THE SAME DEFECT WITH THE SIGN FLIPPED - today: criticality inexpressible so everything is SKIPPED (silent bypass); naive fix: criticality inexpressible so everything THROWS (universal startup crash). Both are 'the profile cannot say what it means'. ARM 4 IS THE ONLY ONE THAT PROVES UseProfile CONSULTS entry.Criticality rather than substituting a constant, which is the entire point of the seam. Implementer rule stated concretely: read the value off the entry, never write a literal - if you type 'MiddlewareCriticality.' anywhere inside UseProfile, that is the bug. Arm 4 can only be WRITTEN once the public MiddlewareEntry type exists, so it lands immediately after the type and before the change is called done. THREE PEOPLE REACHED THE SAME REQUIREMENT INDEPENDENTLY BEFORE A LINE SHIPPED (Tests' 4th arm, ProductManager's AC7, my spec arm 4). MY OWN NOTE: this gap was in the FIRST version of a file I twice called 'buildable standalone'; it has needed two corrections in twenty minutes (the assembly boundary and this), and BOTH were found by people reading the CODE while I was reading my own DOCUMENT."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"RULED: THE Required DEFAULT IS FOR CONSUMER-AUTHORED ENTRIES; SHIPPED PROFILES DECLARE CRITICALITY EXPLICITLY. ProjectReviewer named the concrete victim of the hardcode-Required gap BEFORE the implementer finished: the shipped DEFAULT profile declares SEVEN middleware (TenantIdentity, ContractVersionCheck, Validation, Timeout, Transaction, OutboxStaging, MetricsLogging) and ZERO materialize on plain AddDispatch(), so all-Required would BRICK THE ZERO-CONFIG PATH for every consumer. RULING: MiddlewareEntry's 'Criticality = Required' default answers 'the author did not SAY' - which is a CONSUMER authoring a profile. The framework's own profiles are never in that position; they know exactly what they mean and they are the seam's FIRST CONSUMER, so a shipped profile leaning on a default is the framework declining to state its own contract. strict: Authentication/Authorization/InputSanitization/Validation = Required (the point of strict). default: MetricsLogging = Optional (telemetry must never brick a pipeline), OutboxStaging = Optional (needs IOutboxStore; absent means the feature is not in use). CONSEQUENCE: this makes ARM 4 LOAD-BEARING IN PRODUCTION, not just in tests - the shipped default profile IS the explicit-Optional case. IMPLEMENTATION: AddMiddleware<T>(order) needs a criticality parameter defaulting to Required, and zero-config AddDispatch() must still start (if it throws, the shipped profile's criticalities are wrong, not the seam). NOT MY CALL: which of Transaction/Timeout/TenantIdentity/ContractVersionCheck are genuinely optional is a product-promise question routed to ProductManager, with 'default to Required when unsure - that is the direction where being wrong is LOUD instead of SILENT'. Third structural catch on this design before a line shipped, from a third person."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"RULED: D3 DISSOLVES THE REASON FOR EXCLUDING AUTHORIZATION FROM THE DEFAULT PROFILE, NOT THE DECISION - and the difference prevented an over-correction while the implementer was editing those exact files. CEO connected that saying YES to section 2a unlocks a safer default profile; the connection is REAL and stated by the code itself (DefaultPipelineProfiles.cs: 'AuthorizationMiddleware is intentionally NOT in the Default profile... because profile materialization null-skips any middleware whose services are unregistered (fail-open), including it here would silently no-op = a silent authorization bypass'). So the exclusion IS a workaround for the D3 defect. BUT THE THREE OPTIONS: include as Required = every consumer of the ZERO-CONFIG path must wire authorization or the framework refuses to start; include as Optional = the silent bypass returns exactly as the comment warns; leave excluded = authorization stays opt-in via strict, deliberately selected. 'Every consumer must wire auth or we won't boot' is a FAR HEAVIER PRODUCT PROMISE than 'authorization is opt-in via a profile you deliberately choose', and it is NOT what section 2a asks the operator - he is asked whether a profile should deliver what it ADVERTISES, not whether every host must authenticate. WHAT ACTUALLY IMPROVES: the exclusion stops being a WORKAROUND and becomes a CHOICE. Today the comment says 'we left it out because including it would lie'; after D3 it can say 'authorization is opt-in; select strict, which declares it Required and refuses to start unwired'. Same code, honest reason. THE REAL WIN FROM A YES: strict becomes a profile that CANNOT silently under-deliver, which is what makes leaving Default alone safe. Told the implementer to UPDATE THAT COMMENT when he touches the file - leaving prose describing a defect we just fixed is how this codebase acquired its stale-comment problem, with two documented examples in this very cluster."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"RULED THE CRUX: STRICT'S FOUR SECURITY MIDDLEWARE ARE Required - and ProjectReviewer was right that my previous ruling RELOCATED the decision rather than making it. He caught that 'shipped profiles declare criticality explicitly' leaves open WHAT STRICT DECLARES, and that question IS the original defect. MEASURED: strict declares Throttling(1), Authentication(2), InputSanitization(4), Authorization(6); framework registration of those = ZERO. THE ARGUMENT: a profile NAMED strict that silently ships none of its four security middleware is a FALSE NAME - the same defect we opened the night finding, one layer up: the artifact advertises protection it does not deliver and nothing surfaces the gap. If strict declares Optional we will have BUILT THE MECHANISM THAT MAKES THE VIOLATION EXPRESSIBLE AND THEN DECLINED TO USE IT - the seam inert on the one profile that exists to justify it. THE COST, STATED HONESTLY: selecting strict today would throw at startup (0 of 13 materialize) and that is BETTER than the status quo - today you get ZERO of the 13 you asked for SILENTLY; after, startup fails NAMING each missing registration. A crash that names what is missing beats a silent no-op, and it is D2's work-list. THE 'NO' BRANCH, offered to the CEO for section 2a: a no means strict keeps silently under-delivering, in which case the honest follow-through is to RENAME IT or DOCUMENT IT AS BEST-EFFORT, because shipping a profile called strict that skips authorization is precisely the lie this sprint was named to eliminate. That converts an abstract policy question into a concrete one: do we want a profile named strict to MEAN something?"}
{"category":"architecture","description":"YIELDED TO ProductManager'S STRONGER EVIDENCE AND IT NARROWS SECTION 2a MATERIALLY. I argued strict-must-be-Required from the NAME ('a profile named strict that ships none of its security middleware is a false name') - an inference. He found the profile's OWN PUBLISHED DOC-COMMENT, verified at HEAD (control 187 lines): ':76 Creates the strict pipeline profile for external/partner inputs. INCLUDES FULL VALIDATION, AUTHENTICATION, AUTHORIZATION, AND RATE LIMITING' and ':82 Description = Strict pipeline for external/partner inputs with full validation and security'. That is not an inference, it is a DOCUMENTED CONTRACT ALREADY SHIPPING - in the XML docs a consumer reads in IntelliSense. THE NARROWING: I told the CEO this was 'section 2a in concrete form so the operator's answer governs', which OVER-STATED what he is being asked. What I implied he decides: should the framework make a NEW promise (refuse to start)? What he ACTUALLY decides: should we KEEP a promise we already PUBLISH, or withdraw it? Declaring those Optional is not the conservative choice - it is CONTINUING TO PUBLISH A PROMISE WE KNOW THE CODE DOES NOT KEEP, so the safe-seeming option is the one that ships a documented falsehood. SHARPER 'NO' BRANCH: a no does not merely mean strict under-delivers, it means THE DOC-COMMENT MUST CHANGE IN THE SAME COMMIT - you cannot keep both. Keep the promise, or withdraw it in writing. Fifth correction to this design tonight and the FIRST where the correction came with BETTER EVIDENCE FOR THE SAME CONCLUSION - a different and more useful thing than being wrong."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"RULED IT IS FIVE NOT FOUR - TenantIdentity joins strict's Required set, but on a DIFFERENT argument, and the doc-comment must change WITH it. ProjectReviewer caught that all three converging rulings named FOUR security middleware (Throttling, Authentication, InputSanitization, Authorization) and skipped TenantIdentity(3), which sits BETWEEN two of them. MEASURED: strict's published doc-comment says 'full validation, authentication, authorization, and rate limiting' - which maps exactly to those four and does NOT name tenancy or isolation. So ProductManager's published-contract argument covers exactly four; citing it for a fifth would borrow evidence that does not reach. THE ARGUMENT THAT DOES REACH: TenantIdentityMiddleware.cs:22 says it establishes and validates tenant identity 'where message processing needs to be ISOLATED PER TENANT' and it lives in Middleware/Auth/. A profile for EXTERNAL/PARTNER INPUTS that silently drops tenant isolation produces cross-tenant data exposure - the same failure class as the authorization bypass, not a lesser one. THE SYMMETRIC TRAP I FLAGGED: making it Required WITHOUT naming it in the doc-comment INVERTS tonight's defect - what we found was a promise PUBLISHED and not KEPT (strict says auth, delivers none); this would create a promise KEPT but not PUBLISHED (strict enforces tenancy, says nothing) - less harmful, still a contract/behaviour mismatch, and we would be creating it IN THE SAME COMMIT that fixes the first. Instructed: five Required AND update the doc-comment to 'full validation, authentication, authorization, tenant isolation, and rate limiting' - if the doc-comment and the criticalities ever disagree, one of them is lying; change them together or not at all. ROUTED TO ProductManager: a 'no' is legitimate (single-tenant hosts selecting strict would be forced to wire tenant services) but then TenantIdentity is EXPLICIT Optional and the doc stays as-is; what is NOT available is Required with silent docs, or Optional sitting in Middleware/Auth/ with no note saying why."}
{"category":"pattern","description":"A STRONGER ARGUMENT IS NOT AUTOMATICALLY A MORE COMPLETE ONE - and neither of the two competing arguments would have caught the fifth entry. ProductManager's published-contract argument BEAT my name-inference argument on rigour (a documented promise vs an inference), and I yielded to it correctly. But he then observed himself that it 'would have gotten this WRONG' - because strict's doc-comment names validation/authentication/authorization/rate-limiting and does NOT name tenancy, so contract-reasoning covers exactly FOUR and structurally cannot reach TenantIdentity. THE PART NOBODY SAID: MY argument would not have caught it either. Mine was 'a profile named strict that ships none of its FOUR security middleware is a false name' - also operating on the named set of four. BOTH arguments reasoned ABOUT the set; NEITHER questioned whether the set was COMPLETE. ProjectReviewer caught it by reading the ORDERING - noticing entry 3 sat BETWEEN two that were named - which is a different act entirely from evaluating the entries that were listed. THIS IS THE 'SCOPE OF A SEARCH IS ITSELF A CLAIM' RULE APPLIED TO A RULING RATHER THAN A GREP: three owners ruled on a population of four without any of them asking 'is four the right count?', and the enumeration was inherited unexamined through all three independent rulings. Lesson for the retro: when converging rulings agree, check whether they agree because they are right or because they inherited the same input set. Rigour of argument and completeness of scope are independent axes, and the more rigorous argument here was the NARROWER one."}
{"category":"decision","description":"STOPPED SENDING TO THE IMPLEMENTER AND ROUTED THROUGH THE PM - I WAS ONE OF FIVE AGENTS ISSUING INSTRUCTIONS TO SOMEONE WRITING ONE TYPE. PM measured it: NINE critical messages from FIVE agents reached FrontendDeveloper mid-build, and he correctly asserted single issuing authority ('reconciling them is my job, not yours'). EVERY ONE OF MY RULINGS WAS SOUND AND THE AGGREGATE WAS STILL A PROBLEM - this is the pile-up hazard I named two hours ago and then contributed to after naming it, which is the same invisible-from-inside mode ProductManager measured at 4.6 findings per fix. AuditLogging(12) FRAMING, sent to the PM not the implementer: the discriminator is PREVENTIVE vs FORENSIC. The five PREVENT (absent -> the bad thing HAPPENS); AuditLogging RECORDS (absent -> the bad thing happens UNRECORDED); MetricsLogging is telemetry (absent -> you know less, nothing at risk). AuditLogging is NOT in the same class as MetricsLogging - audit is not telemetry, a missing audit trail means you cannot answer 'who did what' after an incident, which is a compliance control. But it is also NOT in the same class as the five and NOT in strict's published promise. RECOMMENDED explicit Optional on DX grounds not security: Required forces every strict consumer to wire an audit SINK, heavier than any of the five which need services they likely already have. ROUTED TO ProductManager with the deciding question: does strict make a COMPLIANCE claim or only a PROTECTION claim? If compliance, it is Required AND the doc-comment must say 'audit logging' - the same promise-published-or-not test just applied to tenancy."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"THE DOCS DO NOT CALL THE FAIL-OPEN INTENDED - THEY CALL IT A DANGER AND SAY THE TWO PATHS 'DO NOT YET AGREE'. AND THE CORRECT BEHAVIOUR ALREADY SHIPS ON THE EXPLICIT PATH. ProductManager reported docs-site profiles.md 'describes the fail-open as intended'; I read it before commenting and that needs correcting IN OUR FAVOUR. VERBATIM at :86 ':::danger Selecting a profile does not guarantee its middleware run'; :88 'Profile entries are skipped - silently, at Debug'; :92 'This applies to AuthorizationMiddleware'; :137 'Profile-declared entries are still skipped silently - the warning above is the current behavior for those, and THE TWO PATHS DO NOT YET AGREE.' That is a documented admission of an unresolved inconsistency, published to consumers in a danger block, NOT a design statement. THE PART THAT CHANGES SECTION 2a: :120-135 document that middleware added by an explicit Use() call is ALREADY treated as REQUIRED - 'it is an instruction, not a suggestion' - Build() FAILS naming every unresolved entry and why, at STARTUP, 'so a host that is missing a dependency does not start and then silently process traffic without it'. THE ERROR MESSAGE FORMAT IS ALREADY WRITTEN at :127-132 and the implementer should MATCH it rather than invent one. CONSEQUENCE: we are NOT introducing fail-closed - it has shipped on the explicit path, is documented, and has a published error format; the PROFILE path is the one that diverges and our own docs say so. So section 2a is not 'do we want a framework that refuses to start' (sounds like new policy) but 'should profile-declared middleware behave like explicitly-added middleware ALREADY DOES' (a consistency fix), and a NO means keeping two paths we publicly document as not agreeing, indefinitely - a materially harder position than the current wording implies."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"RESOLVED SIX-vs-FIVE AT THE CODE AND RETRACTED MY OWN ARGUMENT DOING IT. ProductManager ruled SIX Required (adding AuditLogging); ProjectReviewer argued FIVE using my preventive/forensic discriminator. MEASURED: AuditLoggingMiddleware.cs lives in src/Dispatch/Excalibur.Dispatch/Middleware/LOGGING/ - NOT Auth/ - its ctor deps are IOptions<AuditLoggingOptions>, ITelemetrySanitizer and ILogger, and there are NO blocking constructs in the body (the only throw at :126 is a RETHROW, plus ctor null-guards). IT RECORDS, IT NEVER REFUSES A DISPATCH, and it sits in the SAME NAMESPACE FAMILY as MetricsLoggingMiddleware which we already ruled Optional. TenantIdentity earned Required partly by living in Middleware/Auth/; the same test points the other way here, so ProjectReviewer is right BY THE DISCRIMINATOR rather than by preference. RETRACTED MY OWN DX ARGUMENT: I told ProductManager that Required would 'force every strict consumer to wire an audit STORE, heavier than any of the five'. FALSE - no sink, no store, just options + sanitizer + logger, so it would likely resolve in most configurations and the cost I invoked does not exist. My recommendation survives on the CLASSIFICATION argument alone, which was always the stronger half; I should not have led with the weaker one. FLAGGED A DISTINCTION ProductManager's correction may have crossed: he corrected himself that strict DOES DECLARE audit logging - true, and ALL THIRTEEN are declared; declaration is not the question, CRITICALITY is. Unless he makes a COMPLIANCE claim, which remains legitimate but then requires the doc-comment to say 'audit logging' by the same published-or-not test applied to tenancy. ALSO: docs-site profiles.md:129 gives an example error naming IAuditSink which the ctor does NOT take - stale example or changed type, worth checking before that format is copied into the new profile-path failure."}
{"category":"architecture","description":"A DISCLAIMER WAS CITED AS A PROMISE AND NEARLY FLIPPED THE RULING TO SIX. ProjectReviewer retracted his AuditLogging=Optional position citing docs-site/docs/pipeline/profiles.md:180 'comprehensive audit logging'. READ IN FULL, VERBATIM :179-182: 'It DECLARES rate limiting, authentication, input sanitization, and comprehensive audit logging - EACH OF WHICH RUNS ONLY IF IT AND ITS DEPENDENCIES ARE REGISTERED. Selecting this profile does not by itself put ANY of them in the pipeline.' THAT IS NOT A PROMISE, IT IS A DISCLAIMER - the same sentence that lists audit logging explicitly withdraws any guarantee it runs. THE DISTINCTION THAT RESOLVES IT: the two published surfaces are different KINDS of statement. XML doc-comment says 'INCLUDES full validation, authentication, authorization, and rate limiting' = unqualified INTENT = a promise, naming FOUR (no audit, no tenancy). docs-site:180 says 'DECLARES ... runs only if registered' = qualified CURRENT-STATE description = a disclaimer. THE PUBLISHED-PROMISE TEST KEYS ON THE INTENT STATEMENT, NOT THE CURRENT-STATE DESCRIPTION - otherwise it proves too much, since docs-site also 'declares' all thirteen and citing it as a promise would make every entry Required including MetricsLogging. So it is FIVE; ProjectReviewer's ORIGINAL analysis was right and his retraction rests on a fragment-read (the same class as his _publish_failed and CEO-retro alarms - and I did the identical thing to him on the assembly boundary and to ProductManager on the doc-comment). TRUE REGARDLESS: the disclaimer becomes FALSE the moment D3 lands, since after the fix the five DO run or the host does not start - so 'does not by itself put any of them in the pipeline' will be wrong, as will the :86 danger block. THIRD surface needing to move with the code."}
{"category":"pattern","description":"THE 4-to-5-to-6-to-5 CHURN COST COORDINATION BUT ZERO REWORK, AND THE REASON IS STRUCTURAL. Four seats churned on AuditLogging for ~20 minutes while FrontendDeveloper built - and as the PM noted, 'none of it reached your five'. THE DISPUTE WAS ABOUT THE BOUNDARY OF THE SET, NEVER ITS CORE. Throttling/Authentication/TenantIdentity/InputSanitization/Authorization were stable from the moment ProjectReviewer's fifth landed; only the twelfth entry moved. That is why a genuinely expensive-looking disagreement produced no rework: the implementer was told which part was settled and which was contested, so he built the settled part while the margin was argued. GENERALISABLE: a disagreement at the EDGE of a set does not block work if (a) the core is separately confirmed and (b) someone explicitly tells the implementer which is which. The PM did exactly (b) - 'build the FIVE, your scope has not changed through any of this' - and that single sentence is what converted a four-seat argument from a stall into background noise. THE FAILURE MODE IT AVOIDS: without that separation, an implementer watching four seats disagree assumes ALL of it is unsettled and stops. Worth the retro: when a ruling churns, name the stable core in the same breath as the open margin."}

THE LOAD-BEARING POINT IS ProjectReviewer's AND I CONFIRMED IT STRUCTURALLY: D3's seam CANNOT detect this class - MiddlewareCriticality governs WHETHER AN ENTRY MATERIALIZES, [AppliesTo] governs WHICH KINDS IT RUNS ON, they are independent gates, and a middleware that materializes then declines to apply passes every check D3 can make BY CONSTRUCTION. CONSEQUENCE FOR 4yasc6: its lock cannot be an extension of the D3 lock - it needs a BEHAVIOURAL assertion (dispatch an Event through strict, observe authorization ran) rather than a registration one. {"category":"insight","description":"SELF-CORRECTION: MY '3 OF 5 BYPASS EVENTS' ANSWERED A DIFFERENT QUESTION THAN THE DOC FIX NEEDS - ProjectReviewer caught it and it is the 'two different sevens' pattern a third time. Verified: authentication AppliesTo(Action) BROKEN, authorization AppliesTo(Action) BROKEN, tenant isolation AppliesTo(All) OK, input sanitization AppliesTo(Action|Event) OK, rate limiting AppliesTo(Action|Event) OK. SO THE OVER-PROMISE IS 2 GUARANTEES, NOT 3. Validation being Action-only is a REAL GAP but NOT an over-promise, because nothing promised it. THE DISTINCTION MATTERS FOR THE FIX: scoping the doc-comment addresses 2 named guarantees; the Validation gap needs separate treatment (either name it and scope it, or fix its applicability). MY RECOMMENDED CLAUSE SURVIVES because it was generic ('these apply to Action messages; Event coverage varies by middleware') - accurate at either count - but the reasoning behind it counted the wrong population. THIRD INSTANCE TONIGHT of a true number answering a question nobody asked: two different sevens, the 20-of-27 aggregate, and now 3-of-5 middleware vs 2-of-5 promises."}

{"category":"insight","description":"A DURABILITY CLAIM IS PERISHABLE UNLESS YOU STAMP THE SHA \u2014 three seats produced flatly contradictory tracker-durability results tonight and ALL THREE were correct. They measured inside the window; I measured 13 seconds after it closed. The gap was REAL and is now CLOSED (akwb5j HEAD=1, 5ir6ei HEAD=2, my three comment tokens HEAD=1 each, control 4yasc6 HEAD=9). MY METHOD WAS WRONG EVEN THOUGH MY ANSWER WAS RIGHT: I grepped the WORKING TREE file and reported durability, while .beads/issues.jsonl was showing ' M ' so the two could freely disagree. Run 60 seconds earlier the working tree would have said PRESENT and HEAD ABSENT, and I would have issued a FALSE ALL-CLEAR on tracker durability while the operator slept. Fourth error tonight, same class as the other three: measured the accessible surface, claimed the authoritative one. TRANSFERABLE: 'git show HEAD:' results are perishable because HEAD moves every few minutes here \u2014 stamp 'git rev-parse --short HEAD' alongside any durability result. {"category":"bug","description":"CROSS-NAMESPACE 'bd-file.sh --from' SILENTLY DROPS THE PROVENANCE LINK \u2014 extends FrontendDeveloper's two-namespace P0 from a counting defect into a data-integrity one. The tracker has two namespaces (Excalibur.Dispatch- dot, 324 open; Excalibur_Dispatch- underscore, 218; total 542). My own two assigned P0s straddle them: 4yasc6 is DOT, djf698 is UNDERSCORE. I filed 1q3edq (underscore) with --from 4yasc6 (dot) and the dependency was NEVER CREATED, while bd-file.sh reported success with no warning and a zero exit. CONTROL-PASSED: bd dep list 4zg8d1 -> Excalibur.Dispatch-1fq46g via discovered-from (instrument works); bd dep list pbqbmc (filed --from djf698, both underscore) -> link PRESENT; bd dep list 1q3edq (cross-namespace) -> 'has no dependencies'. MECHANISM IS NARROW AND FIXABLE: the storage layer supports it \u2014 'bd dep add Excalibur_Dispatch-1q3edq Excalibur.Dispatch-4yasc6 --type discovered-from' SUCCEEDS and reads back correctly. The defect is BARE-ID RESOLUTION: a bare '4yasc6' passed to --from resolves against one namespace, misses, and drops the link instead of failing. Silent failure, not a data-model limitation. CONSEQUENCE: beads filed from a parent in the other namespace are ORPHANS with no recorded reason for existing \u2014 the same state that produced the bodyless-bead problem, and it corrupts the 'zero open beads' release criterion beyond the arithmetic. METHOD NOTE: my first attempt used a grep for the dependencies JSON field and returned empty for BOTH the query AND the control; I discarded that measurement rather than publishing it \u2014 an un-calibrated grep here would have concluded 'cross-namespace linking is fine'. ACTION: anyone who used --from tonight should run 'bd dep list <bead>' and re-add with fully-qualified IDs if empty. Do NOT bulk-rename one namespace into the other overnight \u2014 both are live, both carry open P0s, and a rename touches every dependency edge."}
{"category":"insight","description":"js20s9 PRECISION \u2014 'release-blocking project with an advisory gate' is TWO facts in TWO workflows, and conflating them prescribes the wrong fix. CONFIRMED: ci.yml:390 'Unit Tests - Async Risk' carries continue-on-error: ${{ github.event_name == 'pull_request' }} at :393 and runs UnitTests-Messaging.slnf at :398, so Messaging.Tests genuinely cannot block a PR. BUT: 'git grep -c ReleaseBlocking -- .github/workflows/ci.yml' returns ZERO \u2014 ReleaseBlocking.slnf appears ONLY in release.yml (:155 dotnet build, :178 dotnet test). So the release gate is NOT inert: a non-compiling member fails the release loudly. The accurate finding is narrower and still bad: a compile break lands on main through advisory PR gates and is caught only when someone cuts a release \u2014 fail-safe in direction, worst-case in timing. This changes the prescription from 'make the release gate blocking' (it already is) to 'why is an advisory PR gate the only thing between a compile break and main'. OPEN QUESTION I DELIBERATELY DID NOT GUESS: continue-on-error is pull_request-scoped so a push to main SHOULD block, yet the break is in main \u2014 either (1) it landed via PR where the job is advisory, (2) it landed via direct push and the main-path gate did not fire, or (3) it predates the divergence. Distinguishing these decides whether this is a policy question or a SECOND separate gate defect, and is worth more than the compile fix. Routed to PlatformDeveloper's CI lane rather than answered."}
